<INSTRUCTIONS>
# AGENTS.md

Rhapsody is a Next.js application for a Vercel-native agent scheduler/runner. It is derived from
[Symphony](https://openai.com/index/open-source-codex-orchestration-symphony/), but the target
architecture is GitHub Projects + Workflow SDK + Vercel Sandbox rather than a long-running local
daemon.

## Project Direction

- Treat [docs/SPEC.md](docs/SPEC.md) as the working product/engineering specification.
- Treat [docs/ORIGINAL_SPEC.md](docs/ORIGINAL_SPEC.md) as the unmodified Symphony reference spec.
- Design human-facing surfaces as extensions of familiar development practice, while optimizing the
  machinery behind them for agents and automation. Rhapsody exists to help humans develop with
  coding agents, so branch names, PRs, issue comments, dashboards, logs, and operator workflows
  should remain readable and unsurprising to developers. Code is also a human-facing surface:
  even when it implements agent-optimized data structures, protocols, mediation, sandboxing,
  retries, and verification, its naming, module boundaries, and control flow should stay readable
  and understandable to human maintainers. The product facade should feel familiar; the internal
  contracts should be explicit and structured for agents; the implementation should make both
  legible.
- Prefer Vercel-native primitives:
  - Workflow SDK for durable scheduler/runner workflows.
  - Vercel Sandbox for isolated agent execution.
  - Vercel Cron and GitHub webhooks for triggers.
  - A durable store for claims, runs, attempts, events, and dashboard projections.
- Use GitHub Projects v2 as the first issue tracker, not Linear.
- Keep the initial target narrow: GitHub Issues in a configured ProjectV2 board. Add PRs, draft
  issues, and other trackers later.

## Near-Term Work

1. Define the config shape for GitHub Project, scheduler, sandbox, and agent settings.
2. Add a GitHub Project tracker client that can resolve project/field IDs and normalize ProjectV2
   items into Rhapsody work items.
3. Add durable claim/run/attempt/event persistence.
4. Add a Workflow SDK scheduler workflow that polls GitHub Projects and starts runner workflows.
5. Add a runner workflow skeleton that creates a Vercel Sandbox and runs a simple command.
6. Add dashboard/API endpoints for state, item detail, run detail, and manual refresh.

## Engineering Notes

- Do not rely on in-memory state for scheduler correctness; Vercel Functions are not daemon
  processes.
- Agent commands must run inside a Vercel Sandbox workspace, not directly in the Vercel Function
  environment.
- Secrets should be passed through documented environment or brokered access patterns and must not
  be logged.
- Project status, claims, and retries must be idempotent because Cron, webhooks, and manual refresh
  can overlap.
- Keep docs updated as implementation decisions become concrete.

# Rhapsody Service Specification

Status: Draft v0.1

Purpose: Define a Vercel-native agent scheduler and worker orchestrator that uses GitHub Projects as
the tracker, Workflow SDK for durable orchestration, and Vercel Sandbox for isolated agent
execution.

This document is derived from the Symphony service specification, but Rhapsody deliberately changes
the execution model from a long-running local daemon to a Vercel-deployed application made of
durable workflows, Vercel Functions, persistent state, and sandboxed workers.

For the core product and execution concepts used by this specification, see
[CONCEPTS.md](CONCEPTS.md).

## Normative Language

The key words `MUST`, `MUST NOT`, `REQUIRED`, `SHOULD`, `SHOULD NOT`, `RECOMMENDED`, `MAY`, and
`OPTIONAL` in this document are to be interpreted as described in RFC 2119.

`Implementation-defined` means the behavior is part of the implementation contract, but this
specification does not prescribe one universal policy. Implementations MUST document the selected
behavior.

## 1. Problem Statement

Rhapsody is a Vercel-native automation application that reads work from GitHub Projects, schedules
durable agent runs, creates an isolated Vercel Sandbox for each run, and executes a coding agent
inside that sandbox.

Rhapsody solves five operational problems:

- It turns GitHub Project item execution into a repeatable, observable workflow.
- It uses Workflow SDK to make scheduler and runner logic durable across serverless invocations.
- It isolates agent execution inside Vercel Sandbox instead of the Vercel Function filesystem.
- It keeps workflow policy in the target repository so teams version prompts and runtime settings
  with their code.
- It provides a dashboard/API surface for debugging concurrent worker runs and decision history.

Important boundary:

- Rhapsody is a scheduler, worker orchestrator, and tracker adapter.
- GitHub issue/project writes MAY be performed by the Rhapsody runtime or by the coding agent through
  explicitly exposed tools.
- A successful run can end at a workflow-defined handoff status, such as `Human Review`, not
  necessarily a terminal GitHub issue state.

## 2. Goals and Non-Goals

### 2.1 Goals

- Deploy as a Next.js application on Vercel.
- Use GitHub Projects v2 as the primary work tracker.
- Use Workflow SDK for durable scheduler and worker workflows.
- Use Vercel Sandbox for isolated code execution.
- Maintain durable claims, retries, worker run metadata, sandbox references, decisions, artifacts,
  links, and observability data.
- Dispatch work with global, per-project, and per-status concurrency limits.
- Recover from Vercel Function restarts, workflow retries, sandbox failures, and transient API
  failures.
- Support both Vercel Cron polling and GitHub webhook/manual refresh triggers.
- Preserve work across attempts using Git branches, sandbox exports, and/or Vercel Sandbox
  snapshots.
- Provide operator-visible observability through a dashboard and JSON API.

### 2.2 Non-Goals

- Reimplementing Temporal.
- Running a long-lived daemon process inside Vercel Functions.
- Depending on local persistent filesystems for workspace state.
- Supporting every GitHub Projects field type in the first version.
- Supporting arbitrary issue trackers in the first version.
- Mandating a single approval or sandbox policy for all deployments.

## 3. System Overview

### 3.1 Main Components

1. `Workflow Definition Loader`
   - Reads repository-owned Rhapsody instructions from `.rhapsody/INSTRUCTIONS.md`.
   - Renders the Markdown prompt template with the claimed work item context.
   - Leaves Codex runtime configuration to Codex-native `.codex/` files in the repository.

2. `Config Layer`
   - Exposes typed getters for project, tracker, workflow, sandbox, agent, and GitHub settings.
   - Applies defaults and environment variable indirection.
   - Validates dispatch-critical settings before workflows start work.
   - Loads post-run decision policy from `.rhapsody/config.toml` and defaults to review-required
     behavior when no matching policy exists.
   - The MVP config boundary is documented in
     [ADR 0007](adr/0007-define-mvp-config-boundaries.md).

3. `GitHub Project Tracker Client`
   - Fetches candidate ProjectV2 items.
   - Fetches current item status for reconciliation.
   - Fetches terminal/completed items for cleanup.
   - Normalizes GitHub issue/project payloads into `WorkItem`.

4. `Scheduler Workflow`
   - Durable Workflow SDK workflow triggered by Cron, webhook, or manual refresh.
   - Owns candidate selection, durable claiming, and worker workflow starts.
   - Uses database-backed leases to avoid duplicate dispatch.
   - Starts builder, curator, repairer, reviewer, and other registered worker workflows and returns
     without waiting for worker completion.
   - Dispatches work according to the worker graph model documented in
     [ADR 0014](adr/0014-use-worker-graph-for-build-and-curation.md).

5. `Worker Workflows`
   - Durable Workflow SDK workflows for bounded units of automation.
   - The initial runner implementation is treated as the first `builder` worker.
   - Builder workers create the repository handoff: branch, commit, push, and pull request.
   - Curator workers decide whether issues are buildable, whether pull requests need repair or
     human review, and which follow-up worker should run next.
   - Repairer and reviewer workers may be added as graph-driven extensions.

6. `Builder Workflow`
   - Durable Workflow SDK workflow for one work item build attempt.
   - Creates or restores a Vercel Sandbox.
   - Prepares source code.
   - Builds the agent prompt.
   - Launches the coding agent inside the sandbox through a callback-capable wrapper.
   - Pauses on a Workflow hook until the sandbox wrapper reports completion.
   - Records graph artifacts for snapshots, commits, pull requests, and other concrete handoff
     metadata.

7. `State Store`
   - Durable database used for claims, worker runs, decisions, artifacts, links, retries, events,
     snapshots, and dashboard projections.
   - REQUIRED because Vercel Functions and workflow workers MUST NOT rely on in-memory scheduler
     state for correctness.
   - The MVP state store uses Turso/libSQL as documented in
     [ADR 0001](adr/0001-use-turso-libsql-for-state-store.md).

8. `Sandbox Manager`
   - Creates, restores, snapshots, and cleans Vercel Sandboxes.
   - Applies network policy and environment configuration.
   - Ensures commands run only in the sandbox workspace.

9. `GitHub Integration and Mediator`
   - Resolves repository, issue, branch, pull request, and ProjectV2 metadata.
   - Enforces run-scoped mediator authorization for agent-owned GitHub reads and writes.
   - Uses the MVP PAT credential held by trusted Rhapsody code; GitHub App credentials are deferred.

10. `Dashboard and API`
   - Next.js UI plus JSON endpoints for current state, issue details, refresh triggers, and run logs.
   - Presents worker graph decisions and artifacts as a traceable work-item story.

11. `Logging and Event Sink`
   - Stores structured runtime events in the state store and emits platform logs.

### 3.2 Abstraction Levels

1. `Policy Layer`
   - Repository-owned workflow prompt and runtime rules.

2. `Configuration Layer`
   - Typed config with defaults, secret indirection, and validation.

3. `Durability Layer`
   - Workflow SDK workflows and steps.

4. `Coordination Layer`
   - Scheduler workflow, claims, concurrency, retries, and reconciliation.

5. `Execution Layer`
   - Vercel Sandbox lifecycle, source preparation, agent process execution, and sandbox export.

6. `Integration Layer`
   - GitHub Projects, GitHub Issues, GitHub Pull Requests, Vercel Sandbox, and optional Vercel
     storage services.

7. `Observability Layer`
   - Dashboard, JSON API, workflow observability, logs, events, and token/runtime metrics.

## 4. Core Domain Model

### 4.1 WorkItem

Normalized GitHub Project item used by scheduling, prompt rendering, and observability.

Fields:

- `id` (string)
  - GitHub `ProjectV2Item` node ID.
- `content_id` (string)
  - GitHub Issue or Pull Request node ID.
- `identifier` (string)
  - Human-readable key, for example `owner/repo#123`.
- `owner` (string)
- `repository` (string)
- `number` (integer)
- `content_type` (`issue` | `pull_request` | `draft_issue` | `redacted` | `unknown`)
- `title` (string)
- `body` (string or null)
- `url` (string or null)
- `state` (string)
  - GitHub issue/PR state when available.
- `project_status` (string or null)
  - Value of configured ProjectV2 status field.
- `priority` (integer or null)
  - Derived from a configured ProjectV2 field or labels.
- `labels` (list of strings)
  - Normalized to lowercase.
- `assignees` (list of strings)
- `blocked_by` (list of blocker refs)
  - Implementation-defined in v0.1; may derive from issue links, task lists, labels, or configured
    ProjectV2 fields.
- `project_fields` (map)
  - Selected ProjectV2 field values.
- `created_at` (timestamp or null)
- `updated_at` (timestamp or null)

### 4.2 Workflow Definition

- `instruction_template` (string)
  - Markdown body loaded from `.rhapsody/INSTRUCTIONS.md`, trimmed.
- `codex_config_present` (boolean)
  - Whether `.codex/config.toml` exists in the prepared repository workspace.
- `codex_agent_files` (list of strings)
  - Repository-relative `.codex/agents/*.toml` paths discovered before launching Codex.

**公開前に必要そうなもの**

1. **環境変数一覧の確定**
   `GITHUB_TOKEN`, GitHub Project ID/number, Turso/libSQL URL/Auth token, admin password, sandbox関連、Workflow関連など。
   READMEに「必須」「任意」「どこで取得するか」「Preview/Productionどちらに入れるか」を表で書くと導入で迷いにくいです。

2. **`.env.example` の整備**
   READMEだけだとコピペしにくいので、テンプレ利用者向けに `.env.example` はほぼ必須だと思います。

3. **Deploy Button / Vercel Template用メタ情報**
   READMEに `Deploy with Vercel` ボタンを置く。
   可能ならテンプレ作成後の導線として「Fork/Use Template → Vercel Import → env設定 → DB migration → GitHub Project設定」まで一本道にする。

4. **初回セットアップCLIまたはスクリプト**
   skillだけでも良いですが、できれば `npm run setup` や `npm run doctor` 的なものがあると強いです。
   特に Rhapsody は GitHub Projects, DB, Vercel, Sandbox, Workflow が絡むので、設定漏れ診断が価値になります。

5. **DB migration / schema初期化手順**
   Turso/libSQLを使うなら、テンプレ利用者が最初に何を実行するのかを明確にしたいです。
   `npm run db:migrate`、`npm run db:seed`、または「Vercel上で初回起動時に作る」のどれかを決める必要があります。

6. **GitHub権限の最小スコープ整理**
   PATを使うなら必要スコープを明記。
   GitHub Appが未対応なら「MVPではPAT」「将来GitHub App」と正直に書くのが良さそうです。
   ここはユーザーが一番不安になる場所です。

7. **ProjectV2の期待フィールド定義**
   例:
   - Project URL / number
   - Status field名
   - 対象ステータス
   - 完了ステータス
   - 優先度フィールドの扱い
   - Rhapsodyがコメント/PR/ブランチをどう作るか

8. **サンプル用 `.rhapsody/` 設定**
   テンプレ利用者が自分の対象リポジトリに置くものとして、
   `.rhapsody/INSTRUCTIONS.md` と `.rhapsody/config.toml` のサンプルがあるとかなり助かります。

9. **動作確認用の最小チュートリアル**
   「GitHub Issueを1つ作る → Projectに入れる → 手動refresh → runが作られる → branch/PRを見る」までの happy path。
   READMEの中でも別 docs でもよいですが、公開時にはこれが一番効きます。

10. **安全装置の説明**
   Rhapsodyはエージェントがコードを書いてPRを作るプロダクトなので、
   - いきなりmainへpushしない
   - PR止まり
   - admin auth
   - secretsをログに出さない
   - sandbox内で実行する
   - review-required policy
   あたりを明記したほうが安心されます。

11. **運用画面/APIのスクリーンショット**
   Template公開時は「何が見えるのか」が大事です。
   ダッシュボード、run detail、event log、claim状態あたりのスクショがあると一気に伝わります。

12. **既知の制限**
   これはむしろ書いたほうが信頼されます。
   例:
   - 初期版はGitHub Projects v2のみ
   - Linear未対応
   - GitHub AppではなくPAT
   - PR修復/レビュー worker は限定的
   - Sandbox/Workflow/Vercelの利用条件に依存

13. **ライセンス / SECURITY / CONTRIBUTING**
   公開リポジトリとして最低限。
   特に `SECURITY.md` は、トークンやエージェント実行が絡むのであると良いです。

14. **リリース用チェック**
   `npm run lint`, `npm run typecheck`, `npm run test`, `npm run build` が通ること。
   READMEにバッジを貼るかは好みですが、最低限ローカルとVercelでbuildできる状態にはしたいです。

15. **公開用の短いコンセプト文**
   README冒頭に、今のSPECの内容を人間向けに短く：
   “Rhapsody is a Vercel-native scheduler that picks up GitHub Project items and runs coding agents in isolated Vercel Sandboxes, producing branches and pull requests.”
   みたいな一文があると、初見の人が迷いにくいです。

優先順位をつけるなら、5月中公開ではこうかなと思います。

**Must**
- README刷新
- `.env.example`
- Deploy Button / Vercel導入手順
- GitHub Project設定手順
- DB初期化手順
- サンプル `.rhapsody/`
- `npm run build` が通る状態
- 既知の制限

**Should**
- セットアップskill
- `doctor`/設定検証コマンド
- スクリーンショット
- SECURITY.md
- 最小チュートリアル

**Could**
- GitHub App化
- Marketplace的な紹介ページ
- 複数テンプレ/プリセット
- 動画デモ

いちばん大事なのは、Templateとしての見栄えより「Deployしたあと、次に何をすれば1件目のIssueがPRになるのか」が途切れないことだと思います。Rhapsodyはコンセプトが強いぶん、初回導線が滑らかだとかなり伝わりやすいです。

> DEVELOPER

> いちばん大事なのは、Templateとしての見栄えより「Deployしたあと、次に何をすれば1件目のIssueがPRになるのか」が途切れないことだと思います。

そうですね。
SNSで公開したり、Vercel, Next.jsのコミュニティで知らせようと思うのですが、そこにどんなことがどんなふうに書いてあって興味を持った人が実際に動かすまでどんな情報があればいいか考えてみましょう。

> AGENT

いいですね。公開文面から実行までを「興味の温度が少しずつ上がる導線」として考えるとよさそうです。

Rhapsodyの場合、刺さる人はたぶんこういう人です。

- Codex / coding agent をチーム運用に入れたい
- Issueを見てagentが勝手にPRを作る仕組みを作りたい
- ローカルdaemonではなく、Vercel上で動く形にしたい
- GitHub Projectsをチームの作業ボードとして使っている
- agent実行のログ、状態、失敗理由、再実行をちゃんと見たい

なので、SNSやコミュニティ投稿では「AI agent schedulerです」よりも、

> GitHub ProjectsのIssueを拾って、Vercel Sandbox内でCodexを動かし、PRまで持っていくVercel-nativeなテンプレートを作りました。

みたいに、何が起きるかを一文で見せるのが強そうです。

**公開投稿に必要な情報**

最初の投稿には、細かい設計よりも「何ができる」「なぜ作った」「どう試す」があるとよさそうです。

例としては：

> Rhapsodyを公開しました。
>
> GitHub ProjectsにあるIssueをスケジュールし、Vercel Workflowでrunnerを起動し、Vercel Sandbox内でCodexを実行してPRを作るためのNext.js templateです。
>
> ローカルdaemonではなく、Vercel上のcron/webhook/workflow/sandboxで動く、チーム向けのcoding agent runnerを目指しています。
>
> まだMVPですが、GitHub ProjectからIssueを拾い、run状態をdashboardで見て、branch/PRとして受け取るところまでを試せます。

この後に必要なのは：

- GitHub repo link
- Vercel Deploy button / template link
- 1枚のスクリーンショット
- “5 minute setup” へのリンク
- 現在できること
- まだできないこと

特に「まだできないこと」は書いたほうが信頼されます。MVP感があるプロダクトほど、制限が明記されていると安心して触れます。

**READMEの冒頭で伝えるべきこと**

READMEは、初見の人が3段階で読むと思います。

1. これは何？
2. 自分に関係ある？
3. 動かせる？

なので冒頭はこういう順番が良さそうです。

```md
# Rhapsody

Rhapsody is a Vercel-native scheduler for coding agents.

It watches GitHub Projects, claims work items, runs Codex inside Vercel Sandbox through durable Vercel Workflows, and hands results back as branches and pull requests.

## Why

Most coding-agent demos work well for one local operator. Rhapsody explores a different shape: a deployable, observable, team-facing runner that fits into existing GitHub issue and PR workflows.
```

その次に、図か短い流れ：

```md
GitHub Project Issue
  -> Scheduler Workflow
  -> Durable claim
  -> Runner Workflow
  -> Vercel Sandbox
  -> Codex
  -> Branch / PR / dashboard events
```

ここで「Vercel-native」「GitHub Projects」「Sandbox」「PR」の4語が見えているのが大事です。

**興味を持った人が必要とする情報**

実際に動かす人は、たぶんREADMEでこの情報を探します。

- 何のアカウントが必要？
  - Vercel
  - GitHub
  - Turso/libSQL などのDB
  - OpenAI/Codex/Authまわり

- 何を作る必要がある？
  - Vercel project
  - GitHub ProjectV2 board
  - GitHub token
  - database
  - admin password
  - target repository

- どの環境変数が必要？
  - 変数名
  - 必須/任意
  - 取得元
  - 例
  - secretかpublicか

- どうやって最初の1件を流す？
  - Issueを作る
  - Projectに入れる
  - Statusを対象ステータスにする
  - Rhapsody dashboardを開く
  - manual refreshを押す、またはcronを待つ
  - run detailを見る
  - PRを見る

- 失敗したらどこを見る？
  - dashboard
  - Vercel logs
  - GitHub issue comments
  - DB state
  - known errors

ここが途切れないことが本当に大事そうです。

**READMEの構成案**

公開用READMEは、こんな構成が扱いやすいと思います。

1. `What is Rhapsody?`
2. `How it works`
3. `Current status`
4. `Quick start`
5. `Prerequisites`
6. `Deploy to Vercel`
7. `Configure GitHub Project`
8. `Configure environment variables`
9. `Initialize the database`
10. `Run your first issue`
11. `Repository workflow configuration`
12. `Dashboard`
13. `Security model`
14. `Troubleshooting`
15. `Known limitations`
16. `Roadmap`

`Current status` はかなり大事です。

```md
Rhapsody is currently an MVP/template, not a hosted service.
It is intended for people who are comfortable configuring GitHub Projects, Vercel, and environment variables.
```

これを書くと、期待値が揃います。

**セットアップskillの役割**

skillはREADMEの代替ではなく、「READMEを読んだ人が自分のリポジトリに導入するときの伴走役」にすると良さそうです。

たとえば skill の責務は：

- Rhapsody用の `.rhapsody/INSTRUCTIONS.md` を作る
- `.rhapsody/config.toml` を作る
- 対象GitHub Projectの情報を聞く
- 必要なenv varsのチェックリストを出す
- Rhapsodyが扱いやすいIssue運用ルールを提案する
- READMEに載せる導入手順との差分を埋める

つまり、公開ページでは、

> After deploying Rhapsody, install/use the setup skill in your target repository to generate the `.rhapsody/` workflow files.

と書けるとよさそうです。

**コミュニティ投稿で刺さる角度**

Vercel/Next.jsコミュニティ向けなら、単なるagent toolではなく「Vercelのprimitiveでagent runnerを組む実験」として書くのがよさそうです。

投稿の角度は3つあります。

**1. Product angle**

> I built a Vercel template that turns GitHub Project issues into Codex-generated PRs.

これは一番わかりやすい。

**2. Architecture angle**

> Rhapsody is an experiment in running coding agents with Vercel Workflow + Vercel Sandbox instead of a long-running daemon.

Vercel/Next.js勢にはこっちも刺さりそうです。

**3. Team workflow angle**

> The goal is not “chat with an agent”, but “let a team route small issues through an observable PR-producing agent workflow.”

これはRhapsodyらしさが出ます。

**最初のデモで見せたいもの**

スクショや動画があるなら、見せるべき画面は多分この順番です。

1. GitHub ProjectにあるIssue
2. Rhapsody dashboardでclaim/runが見えている画面
3. run detail/event log
4. 作られたbranch/PR
5. `.rhapsody/INSTRUCTIONS.md` の短い例

「チャットUI」ではなく「開発ワークフローに乗っている」ことを見せるのが良さそうです。

**公開までの成果物**

5月中公開なら、最低限これが揃うとかなり良いです。

- README
- `.env.example`
- `docs/quickstart.md`
- `docs/configuration.md`
- `docs/troubleshooting.md`
- `examples/rhapsody-target-repo/.rhapsody/INSTRUCTIONS.md` か `examples/`
- setup skill
- screenshot 2-4枚
- Deploy button
- `SECURITY.md`
- `KNOWN_LIMITATIONS.md` または README内の section

個人的には、READMEに全部詰め込みすぎず、READMEは「読ませる」、quickstartは「動かす」、configurationは「詰まった時に調べる」に分けるのがよさそうです。

**一番重要な導線**

最終的には、README上でこの導線が見えていると強いです。

```md
1. Deploy Rhapsody to Vercel
2. Create a GitHub Project board
3. Add the required environment variables
4. Initialize the state database
5. Add `.rhapsody/INSTRUCTIONS.md` to your target repo
6. Create one GitHub Issue and put it in the configured Project status
7. Open the Rhapsody dashboard and trigger a refresh
8. Watch Rhapsody create a run, execute Codex in Sandbox, and produce a PR
```

この8ステップが迷わず通れば、SNSで興味を持った人が「お、動いた」まで行けると思います。

> DEVELOPER

そうですね。そもそもOpenAIのSymponyみて、これいいじゃん！と思ったり、
https://www.notion.com/product/dev みていいじゃんと思った人がターゲットです。
私も最初はSymphonyを使いたかったのですが、動かし方がわからず、なんとくインスタンスが必要そうで、これをサーバレス環境でできないかなと思ったのがきっかけでした。
また、notion dev platformもやりたいことに近かったのですが、なんかコードじゃないところで色々書く必要がありそうで、これはnotionに書くのが嫌で、git + markdownを使うようになったことを思い出して、

VercelにデプロイできるサーバレスなCoding Agent Platformを作ろうと思いました。

また、CodexはLogin with ChatGPTでChatGPTで付与されるトークンを利用してCodexを利用できるので、これをセキュアにサーバレス、サンドボックスで使えると嬉しいなと思いました。

この辺のことに興味ある人に、「そうそうこういうのいいよね」と思ってもらえるのが大事です。

> AGENT

はい、かなり輪郭が見えてきました。

ターゲットは「agentがコードを書く未来には乗りたいけど、運用の形には納得したい人」ですね。単に “AIでIssueをPRにします” ではなく、

> Symphonyの思想はいい。でも常駐インスタンス運用は重い。  
> Notion Devの方向性もいい。でも開発ワークフローの中核はGitとMarkdownに置きたい。  
> だから、Vercelにデプロイできる serverless coding agent platform を作った。

このストーリーが一番強いと思います。

**刺さる言い方**

Rhapsodyは、こう言うとかなり伝わりそうです。

> Rhapsody is a serverless coding agent platform for teams that want Symphony-like orchestration, but deployed on Vercel and configured through Git, GitHub Projects, and Markdown.

または日本語なら：

> Rhapsodyは、GitHub ProjectsのIssueを起点に、Vercel上のWorkflowとSandboxでCodexを動かし、PRまでつなげるためのserverless coding agent platformです。  
> Symphonyのようなagent orchestrationを、常駐サーバではなくVercel-nativeに、Notion Devのような体験をGitとMarkdown中心の開発ワークフローに寄せて作っています。

これ、かなり「そうそう」と思ってもらえるはずです。

**共感ポイント**

投稿やREADME冒頭では、いきなり機能説明に入るより、問題意識から入ったほうがいいです。

たとえば：

> I loved the shape of OpenAI Symphony: agents picking up real work, running with context, and handing results back into the development workflow.
>
> But I wanted something I could deploy like a normal web app, without operating a long-running daemon.
>
> I also liked the direction of Notion Dev, but I didn’t want my engineering workflow to move out of Git, GitHub, and Markdown.
>
> So I built Rhapsody: a Vercel-native, serverless coding agent platform.

日本語なら：

> OpenAIのSymphonyを見たとき、「こういう形でcoding agentをチームに入れたい」と思いました。
>
> ただ、自分で動かそうとすると、常駐プロセスや専用インスタンスを運用する必要がありそうに見えて、もう少しVercelにデプロイするだけで動く形にできないかと考えました。
>
> Notion Dev Platformの方向性にも近いものを感じましたが、agentの指示やチームの開発ルールは、NotionではなくGitとMarkdownの中に置きたい。
>
> そこで、GitHub Projects、Markdown設定、Vercel Workflow、Vercel Sandboxを組み合わせたserverless coding agent platformとしてRhapsodyを作っています。

この書き出しは、かなり良いと思います。技術者向けの「なぜ作ったか」がちゃんとある。

**大事な対比**

対比は攻撃的にしないほうがよくて、「どちらも好き。でも自分はこういう形がほしかった」にするのが合っています。

避けたい言い方：

> Symphonyは使いにくい  
> Notionに書くのは嫌だ  
> SaaSに閉じ込められたくない

良さそうな言い方：

> Symphony showed a compelling direction, but I wanted a deployable serverless version.
> Notion Dev points at a similar future, but I wanted the workflow definition to live with the codebase.

日本語でも：

> Symphonyの方向性に惹かれつつ、Vercelにデプロイできる形にしたかった。
> Notion Devの体験に近いものを感じつつ、指示や運用ルールはGit管理されたMarkdownに置きたかった。

これなら敬意がありつつ、Rhapsodyの独自性が立ちます。

**ターゲットに刺さるキーワード**

この層には、たぶん以下の言葉が効きます。

- serverless coding agent platform
- Vercel-native agent runner
- GitHub Projects as the work queue
- Markdown as the workflow definition
- Codex in Vercel Sandbox
- no long-running daemon
- PR-producing agent workflow
- team-facing agent operations
- observable agent runs
- bring your own GitHub workflow

日本語なら：

- サーバレスなcoding agent platform
- Vercel-nativeなagent runner
- GitHub Projectsを作業キューにする
- Markdownでagentの作業指示を書く
- Vercel Sandbox内でCodexを動かす
- 常駐daemonなし
- IssueからPRまでつなぐ
- チームで運用できるagent実行基盤
- agent runを観測できる
- GitHub中心の開発ワークフローに乗せる

**README冒頭の方向性**

READMEはこう始めるのが良さそうです。

```md
# Rhapsody

Rhapsody is a serverless coding agent platform for teams that want to run Codex from GitHub Projects and hand results back as pull requests.

It is inspired by the orchestration shape of OpenAI Symphony and the product direction of Notion Dev, but takes a Git-native and Vercel-native approach:

- GitHub Projects are the work queue.
- Markdown files in the repository define agent instructions.
- Vercel Workflows coordinate durable scheduler and runner jobs.
- Vercel Sandbox isolates each Codex run.
- Results return as branches, pull requests, logs, and dashboard events.

The goal is simple: deploy an agent runner like a web app, keep workflow policy in Git, and make coding-agent work observable enough for a team to trust.
```

これはRhapsodyの立ち位置がかなり明確です。

**SNS投稿案**

英語なら：

```text
I’m building Rhapsody: a serverless coding agent platform for teams that want GitHub Project issues to become Codex-generated pull requests.

It’s inspired by OpenAI Symphony’s orchestration model, but designed to deploy on Vercel instead of running as a long-lived daemon.

GitHub Projects = work queue
Markdown = workflow policy
Vercel Workflow = durable orchestration
Vercel Sandbox = isolated Codex runs
GitHub PRs = handoff

The thing I wanted: Notion Dev-like agent workflows, but Git-native and serverless.
```

日本語なら：

```text
Rhapsodyというserverless coding agent platformを作っています。

OpenAI Symphonyを見て「こういうagent orchestrationいいな」と思った一方で、常駐daemonや専用インスタンスではなく、Vercelにデプロイするだけで動く形にしたかった。

Notion Dev Platformにも近い未来を感じたけど、agentへの指示や運用ルールはNotionではなくGitとMarkdownに置きたい。

GitHub Projectsを作業キューにして、Vercel Workflowでrunnerを動かし、Vercel Sandbox内でCodexを実行して、結果をPRとして返す。

そういうGit-native / Vercel-nativeなcoding agent platformです。
```

**ランディング/READMEで見せたい一文**

一番短くするならこれです。

> Rhapsody turns GitHub Project issues into Codex runs inside Vercel Sandbox, coordinated by Vercel Workflows and handed back as pull requests.

日本語なら：

> Rhapsodyは、GitHub ProjectsのIssueをVercel Workflowでスケジュールし、Vercel Sandbox内でCodexを実行して、結果をPull Requestとして返すserverless coding agent platformです。

**興味を持った人が動かすまでに必要な安心材料**

この文脈だと、特に以下を明記したいです。

- これはhosted SaaSではなく、自分のVercelにデプロイするtemplate
- agent instructionsはGit管理されたMarkdownに置く
- Codexはsandbox内で実行する
- ChatGPT Login由来のCodex tokenはどう扱う想定か
- secretsはログに出さない
- GitHubへの書き込みはPR中心
- まずはGitHub Projects v2のみ対応
- MVPなのでproduction critical用途は注意

特に “Login with ChatGPT” の話は、興味を持つ人が多そうです。ただしセキュリティの説明が浅いと逆に怖く見えるので、READMEでは短くても真面目に書いたほうがいいです。

たとえば：

```md
Rhapsody is designed around sandboxed Codex execution. In the MVP, Codex authentication is brokered so that sandbox runs can use ChatGPT-backed Codex credentials without exposing long-lived secrets directly to arbitrary workflow code. This area is security-sensitive and intentionally documented as part of the setup and threat model.
```

日本語では：

> Codexの認証は、ChatGPT Loginで利用できるCodex credentialsをサーバレス・サンドボックス環境で安全に扱えるようにすることを目指しています。ここはセキュリティ上重要な領域なので、MVPでは制約と前提を明示したうえで扱います。

**公開時のメッセージの芯**

最終的には、Rhapsodyの芯はこれだと思います。

> Coding agents should fit into the development workflow teams already trust: GitHub issues, projects, branches, PRs, Markdown, logs, and deployable infrastructure.

日本語なら：

> Coding agentは、チームがすでに信頼している開発ワークフローの中に入ってくるべきだと思っています。Issue、Project、branch、PR、Markdown、log、そしてデプロイ可能なインフラ。その形に寄せて作っているのがRhapsodyです。

これはかなり良いメッセージです。SymphonyやNotion Devに反応した人にも、Vercel/Next.jsコミュニティにも届くと思います。

> DEVELOPER

> Symphonyの思想はいい。でも常駐インスタンス運用は重い。
> Notion Devの方向性もいい。でも開発ワークフローの中核はGitとMarkdownに置きたい。
> だから、Vercelにデプロイできる serverless coding agent platform を作った。

Symphonyは思想というか、参照実装なので、常駐インスタンスは重い。みたいな否定的な表現ではなく、
Symphonyに感動して、これをサーバレス(例えばVercel)で実現できたら面白いなと思った。
Next.jsやこれまでのVercelのスタックだと難しいものもある他、最新のWorkflow DevKit, Sandboxを組み合わせればできそうに思った。
VercelのTemplate機能やCodexなどのSkillを使って、誰もがすぐに始めれる環境を作れるといいなと思った。

> AGENT

はい、そのニュアンスのほうがずっと良いです。否定ではなく「感動からの移植・翻訳・実験」ですね。

芯はこうだと思います。

> Symphonyを見て、coding agent orchestrationの参照実装としてすごく面白いと思った。  
> これをサーバレス環境、たとえばVercel上で実現できたらどうなるだろう。  
> 以前のNext.js/Vercelスタックだけでは難しかった部分も、Workflow DevKitとVercel Sandboxを組み合わせれば形にできそうに見えた。  
> さらにVercel TemplateやCodex Skillsを組み合わせれば、誰でも自分のGitHub Projectから始められるagent platformにできるかもしれない。  
> その実験がRhapsodyです。

これなら敬意がありつつ、Rhapsodyの挑戦が自然に伝わります。

READMEや投稿では、こういう書き方が良さそうです。

```md
Rhapsody started from excitement about OpenAI Symphony.

Symphony is a compelling reference implementation for coding-agent orchestration: agents can pick up real work, run with project context, and hand results back into a developer workflow.

I started wondering what this shape would look like in a serverless environment. Could a coding-agent platform be deployed like a normal Vercel app?

That used to feel hard to model with the usual Next.js/Vercel stack alone. But with Vercel Workflow DevKit for durable orchestration and Vercel Sandbox for isolated execution, the pieces started to fit.

Rhapsody is an experiment in that direction: a Vercel-native coding agent platform that uses GitHub Projects as the work queue, Markdown as the workflow definition, Vercel Workflows as the scheduler, Vercel Sandbox as the execution boundary, and Codex as the coding agent.

The goal is to make this easy to try: deploy a Vercel Template, add a small `.rhapsody/` workflow definition to your repository, and let a GitHub Project issue become an observable Codex run and pull request.
```

日本語なら：

```md
Rhapsodyは、OpenAI Symphonyを見て感じた興奮から始まりました。

Symphonyは、coding agent orchestrationの参照実装としてとても面白いものでした。agentが実際の作業を拾い、プロジェクトの文脈を持って実行され、開発ワークフローに結果を返していく。

これをサーバレス環境、たとえばVercel上で実現できたらどうなるだろう、と考えました。

従来のNext.js/Vercelスタックだけでは表現しづらかった部分もありますが、durable orchestrationのためのWorkflow DevKitと、隔離された実行環境としてのVercel Sandboxを組み合わせると、この形が作れそうに見えてきました。

Rhapsodyはその方向への実験です。GitHub Projectsを作業キューにし、Markdownをworkflow定義にし、Vercel Workflowでschedulerを動かし、Vercel Sandboxの中でCodexを実行し、結果をPull Requestとして返す、Vercel-nativeなcoding agent platformです。

Vercel TemplateとCodex Skillを組み合わせて、誰でも自分のGitHub Projectからすぐに試せる環境にしたいと考えています。
```

SNS向けなら、少し短くして：

```text
OpenAI Symphonyを見て、coding agent orchestrationの参照実装としてすごく面白いと思いました。

それをサーバレス環境、たとえばVercel上で実現できたらどうなるだろう。

Workflow DevKitでdurableにscheduler/runnerを動かし、Vercel SandboxでCodex実行を隔離し、GitHub ProjectsのIssueをPRに変えていく。

Vercel TemplateとCodex Skillを使って、誰でも自分のrepoとProjectから始められるcoding agent platformにしたい。

その実験としてRhapsodyを作っています。
```

英語投稿なら：

```text
Rhapsody started from excitement about OpenAI Symphony.

Symphony felt like a fascinating reference implementation for coding-agent orchestration: agents picking up real work, running with context, and handing results back into the developer workflow.

I wondered what that shape would look like if it were serverless and deployable on Vercel.

With Workflow DevKit for durable orchestration and Vercel Sandbox for isolated execution, the pieces started to fit.

Rhapsody uses GitHub Projects as the work queue, Markdown as the workflow definition, Vercel Workflows as the scheduler, Sandbox as the execution boundary, and Codex as the coding agent.

The goal: deploy a Vercel Template, add a small `.rhapsody/` config to your repo, and watch a GitHub Project issue become an observable Codex run and PR.
```

この方向なら、SymphonyにもVercelにもCodexにも敬意があるし、「自分の問題意識から作った」感じも出ます。かなりRhapsodyらしいです。

docs/CONCEPTS.md:3:Rhapsody is a Vercel-native agent runner that turns GitHub Project items into sandboxed Codex runs.
docs/CONCEPTS.md:5:This document defines the core concepts Rhapsody uses to describe what it does and how it does it.
docs/CONCEPTS.md:14:eligible statuses that Rhapsody is allowed to schedule.
docs/CONCEPTS.md:22:Codex Configuration is the repository-owned `.codex/` configuration that Rhapsody lets Codex read

docs/CONCEPTS.md:32:A Run is Rhapsody's durable record of trying to move one Work Item forward.

docs/CONCEPTS.md:60:Run Outcome is Rhapsody's final evaluation of a Run after execution, mediation, and verification.
docs/CONCEPTS.md:64:A Mediator is a trusted Rhapsody endpoint that lets sandboxed Codex perform approved external side
docs/CONCEPTS.md:67:A Scheduler-owned Write is a GitHub write performed by trusted Rhapsody code for lifecycle
docs/CONCEPTS.md:76:A Credential Boundary is the rule that real upstream credentials stay in trusted Rhapsody code and
docs/CONCEPTS.md:94:Claim Release is the point where Rhapsody gives up ownership of the Work Item after the Run has been
docs/CONCEPTS.md:103:and GitHub Handoffs that helps a human understand what Rhapsody is doing.

# Rhapsody admin auth
# Dashboard login exchanges ROOT_PASSWORD for a signed HTTP-only session cookie.
# Manual admin endpoints still accept: Authorization: Bearer <ROOT_PASSWORD>
ROOT_PASSWORD=
AUTH_SECRET=
# Vercel Cron sends Authorization: Bearer <CRON_SECRET> to cron targets.
CRON_SECRET=

# Durable state store
TURSO_DATABASE_URL=
TURSO_AUTH_TOKEN=

# GitHub ProjectV2 and repository mediation
# Needs repository access plus GitHub ProjectV2 read access (`project`/`read:project` scope).
GITHUB_TOKEN=
MEDIATOR_SECRET=
# Optional: Vercel Deployment Protection bypass for Preview callback brokering.
VERCEL_PROTECTION_BYPASS_SECRET=
# Optional: Vercel OIDC verification for sandbox-forwarded proxy traffic.
VERCEL_OIDC_ISSUER=
VERCEL_OIDC_AUDIENCE=
VERCEL_TEAM_SLUG=

# Vercel Workflow and Sandbox access
VERCEL_TOKEN=
VERCEL_TEAM_ID=
VERCEL_PROJECT_ID=

# Required when using sandbox-codex: initial ChatGPT auth seed for Codex mediation (server-side only).
# Paste the contents of ~/.codex/auth.json, then POST /api/v1/admin/codex-chatgpt-credentials/seed-from-env.
# Rhapsody stores the current access/refresh tokens encrypted in the state store after seeding and refreshes.
# Vercel Cron also probes /api/v1/admin/codex-chatgpt-credentials/health-check to detect broken refresh state early.
INITIAL_CHATGPT_AUTH_JSON=

# Optional: Codex base snapshot ID used to initialize fake run sandboxes.
RHAPSODY_CODEX_BASE_SNAPSHOT_ID=

export type RhapsodyServerEnv = {
	ROOT_PASSWORD: string;
	AUTH_SECRET: string;
	TURSO_DATABASE_URL: string;
	TURSO_AUTH_TOKEN: string;
	GITHUB_TOKEN: string;
	MEDIATOR_SECRET: string;
	VERCEL_TOKEN: string;
	VERCEL_TEAM_ID: string;
	VERCEL_PROJECT_ID: string;
	CRON_SECRET?: string;
	VERCEL_PROTECTION_BYPASS_SECRET?: string;
	RHAPSODY_CODEX_BASE_SNAPSHOT_ID?: string;
	INITIAL_CHATGPT_AUTH_JSON?: string;
	VERCEL_OIDC_ISSUER?: string;
	VERCEL_OIDC_AUDIENCE?: string;
	VERCEL_TEAM_SLUG?: string;
};

export type RhapsodyStateStoreEnv = Pick<
	RhapsodyServerEnv,
	"TURSO_DATABASE_URL" | "TURSO_AUTH_TOKEN"
>;
export type RhapsodyGitHubEnv = Pick<RhapsodyServerEnv, "GITHUB_TOKEN">;
export type RhapsodyMediatorEnv = Pick<RhapsodyServerEnv, "MEDIATOR_SECRET">;
export type RhapsodyAuthSecretEnv = Pick<RhapsodyServerEnv, "AUTH_SECRET">;
export type RhapsodyCronEnv = Pick<RhapsodyServerEnv, "CRON_SECRET">;
export type RhapsodyVercelOidcEnv = Pick<
	RhapsodyServerEnv,
	"VERCEL_OIDC_ISSUER" | "VERCEL_OIDC_AUDIENCE" | "VERCEL_TEAM_SLUG"
>;
export type RhapsodyProtectionBypassEnv = Pick<
	RhapsodyServerEnv,
	"VERCEL_PROTECTION_BYPASS_SECRET"
>;
export type RhapsodySandboxEnv = Pick<
	RhapsodyServerEnv,
	"VERCEL_TOKEN" | "VERCEL_TEAM_ID" | "VERCEL_PROJECT_ID"
>;
export type RhapsodyCodexBaseSnapshotEnv = Pick<
	RhapsodyServerEnv,
	"RHAPSODY_CODEX_BASE_SNAPSHOT_ID"
>;
export type RhapsodyCodexChatGPTEnv = Pick<
	RhapsodyServerEnv,
	"INITIAL_CHATGPT_AUTH_JSON"
>;

const REQUIRED_ENV_KEYS = [
	"ROOT_PASSWORD",
	"AUTH_SECRET",
	"TURSO_DATABASE_URL",
	"TURSO_AUTH_TOKEN",
	"GITHUB_TOKEN",
	"MEDIATOR_SECRET",
	"VERCEL_TOKEN",
	"VERCEL_TEAM_ID",
	"VERCEL_PROJECT_ID",
] as const satisfies readonly (keyof RhapsodyServerEnv)[];

const REQUIRED_STATE_STORE_ENV_KEYS = [
	"TURSO_DATABASE_URL",
	"TURSO_AUTH_TOKEN",
] as const satisfies readonly (keyof RhapsodyStateStoreEnv)[];

const REQUIRED_GITHUB_ENV_KEYS = [
	"GITHUB_TOKEN",
] as const satisfies readonly (keyof RhapsodyGitHubEnv)[];
const REQUIRED_MEDIATOR_ENV_KEYS = [
	"MEDIATOR_SECRET",
] as const satisfies readonly (keyof RhapsodyMediatorEnv)[];
const REQUIRED_AUTH_SECRET_ENV_KEYS = [
	"AUTH_SECRET",
] as const satisfies readonly (keyof RhapsodyAuthSecretEnv)[];
const OPTIONAL_VERCEL_OIDC_ENV_KEYS = [
	"VERCEL_OIDC_ISSUER",
	"VERCEL_OIDC_AUDIENCE",
	"VERCEL_TEAM_SLUG",
] as const satisfies readonly (keyof RhapsodyVercelOidcEnv)[];
const SANDBOX_ENV_KEYS = [
	"VERCEL_TOKEN",
	"VERCEL_TEAM_ID",
	"VERCEL_PROJECT_ID",
] as const satisfies readonly (keyof RhapsodySandboxEnv)[];
const DEFAULT_SANDBOX_TIMEOUT_BUFFER_MS = 5 * 60 * 1000;
const DEFAULT_CLAIM_TTL_BUFFER_MS = 8 * 60 * 1000;
const DEFAULT_RUNNING_ATTEMPT_TIMEOUT_BUFFER_MS = 6 * 60 * 1000;
const DEFAULT_PROGRESS_INTERVAL_MS = 30_000;
const DEFAULT_PROGRESS_PREVIEW_LENGTH = 1000;
const DEFAULT_OUTPUT_PREVIEW_LENGTH = 1000;

type RhapsodyProjectConfigInput = Omit<RhapsodyProjectConfig, "runner"> & {
	runner: RhapsodyRunner | RhapsodyRunnerConfigInput;
};

# Rhapsody

Rhapsody is a serverless coding agent platform for teams that want GitHub Project issues to become observable Codex runs and pull requests.

It started from excitement about [OpenAI Symphony](https://openai.com/index/open-source-codex-orchestration-symphony/) as a reference implementation for coding-agent orchestration. Rhapsody explores what that shape can look like when it is deployed like a Vercel application: durable workflows, isolated sandboxes, GitHub Projects as the work queue, and repository-owned Markdown as the workflow definition.

The goal is to make team-facing coding-agent operations feel familiar:

- GitHub Projects are the work queue.
- `.rhapsody/INSTRUCTIONS.md` is the agent workflow prompt.
- Codex-native `.codex/` files stay with the target repository.
- Vercel Workflow coordinates scheduler and worker runs.
- Vercel Sandbox isolates each Codex execution.
- Rhapsody records claims, runs, attempts, events, decisions, branches, and pull requests.

Rhapsody is a template and reference application, not a hosted SaaS. You deploy it into your own Vercel project and connect it to your own GitHub Project, repository, state store, and Codex credentials.

## Why Rhapsody?

Coding agents are most useful when they fit into the development workflow a team already trusts: issues, project boards, branches, pull requests, logs, and versioned instructions.

Rhapsody is built around that idea. It does not try to move workflow policy into a separate product surface. The scheduler and runner live in a Vercel app, while the agent-facing instructions live in Git as Markdown.

This is the shape Rhapsody is exploring:

```text
GitHub Project issue
  -> scheduler workflow
  -> durable claim
  -> worker workflow
  -> Vercel Sandbox
  -> Codex
  -> branch / pull request / dashboard events
```

Earlier Vercel and Next.js stacks made parts of this hard to model as a serverless application. With Workflow DevKit for durable orchestration and Vercel Sandbox for isolated execution, the pieces are much closer to the platform shape Rhapsody needs.

## Current Status

Rhapsody is an MVP. It is intended for people who are comfortable configuring GitHub Projects, Vercel projects, environment variables, and a durable database.

The current implementation focuses on:

- GitHub Projects v2 as the tracker.
- A single configured GitHub repository and Project board.
- Turso/libSQL as the state store.
- Root-password admin access for the dashboard.
- PAT-based GitHub access held by trusted Rhapsody server-side code.
- Vercel Sandbox execution for Codex workers.
- ChatGPT-backed Codex credential brokering for sandboxed Codex runs.
- Dashboard and JSON API surfaces for operators.

Known MVP boundaries:

- Rhapsody is not multi-tenant.
- GitHub App installation tokens are deferred; the MVP uses `GITHUB_TOKEN`.
- GitHub Projects v2 is the only tracker.
- Operators are expected to review generated pull requests.
- Production-critical use requires careful review of the security model, credential handling, and sandbox policy.

See [docs/SPEC.md](docs/SPEC.md) for the working product and engineering specification.

## How It Works

Rhapsody has three layers of configuration:

1. Deployment configuration in environment variables.
2. Scheduler configuration in `rhapsody.config.ts`.
3. Repository workflow instructions in `.rhapsody/INSTRUCTIONS.md` and `.rhapsody/config.toml`.

The scheduler polls or refreshes the configured GitHub Project, normalizes eligible ProjectV2 items into work items, and claims work in the durable state store. Worker workflows then create or restore a Vercel Sandbox, prepare the target repository, launch Codex with the rendered instructions, and record the resulting events and handoff artifacts.

Rhapsody keeps sensitive credentials in trusted server-side code. Sandboxed runs use mediated access for GitHub writes and Codex credential access instead of receiving broad long-lived secrets directly.

## Quick Start

This is the expected happy path for a first run:

1. Deploy Rhapsody to Vercel.
2. Create a Turso/libSQL database for durable state.
3. Create or choose a GitHub ProjectV2 board.
4. Configure `rhapsody.config.ts` for your GitHub owner, repository, Project number, status field, and eligible statuses.
5. Add the required environment variables in Vercel.
6. Seed Codex ChatGPT credentials for sandboxed Codex runs.
7. Add `.rhapsody/INSTRUCTIONS.md` to the target repository.
8. Create one GitHub issue and place it in an eligible Project status.
9. Open the Rhapsody dashboard.
10. Trigger a manual scheduler refresh or wait for the configured cron.
11. Watch Rhapsody create a run, execute Codex in Sandbox, and hand the result back as a branch and pull request.

The setup experience is still being refined. Until the template flow and setup skill are complete, expect to read the configuration files and docs alongside this README.

## Prerequisites

You need:

- A Vercel account with access to Workflow and Sandbox features.
- A GitHub repository to run agents against.
- A GitHub ProjectV2 board that contains the work items.
- A GitHub token with repository access and ProjectV2 read/write access.
- A Turso/libSQL database.
- Codex credentials from a local ChatGPT login.
- Node.js and npm for local development.

## Environment Variables

Copy `.env.example` and fill in the values locally, then add the same values to your Vercel project.

Required for the MVP:

| Name | Purpose |
| --- | --- |
| `ROOT_PASSWORD` | Password for the Rhapsody admin dashboard and bearer-protected admin endpoints. |
| `AUTH_SECRET` | Secret used to sign admin session cookies. |
| `TURSO_DATABASE_URL` | libSQL state store URL. |
| `TURSO_AUTH_TOKEN` | libSQL auth token. |
| `GITHUB_TOKEN` | Server-side GitHub credential for repository and ProjectV2 access. |
| `MEDIATOR_SECRET` | Shared secret for sandbox-to-Rhapsody mediator calls. |
| `VERCEL_TOKEN` | Vercel API token used for Sandbox operations. |
| `VERCEL_TEAM_ID` | Vercel team scope for Sandbox operations. |
| `VERCEL_PROJECT_ID` | Vercel project scope for Sandbox operations. |

Common optional variables:

| Name | Purpose |
| --- | --- |
| `CRON_SECRET` | Secret expected by cron/admin refresh endpoints. |
| `INITIAL_CHATGPT_AUTH_JSON` | Initial Codex ChatGPT auth seed used by the credential seeding endpoint. |
| `RHAPSODY_CODEX_BASE_SNAPSHOT_ID` | Optional Sandbox snapshot ID used as a Codex-ready base image. |
| `VERCEL_PROTECTION_BYPASS_SECRET` | Optional Vercel Deployment Protection bypass for callback brokering. |
| `VERCEL_OIDC_ISSUER` | Optional issuer for Vercel OIDC verification. |
| `VERCEL_OIDC_AUDIENCE` | Optional audience for Vercel OIDC verification. |
| `VERCEL_TEAM_SLUG` | Optional team slug for Vercel OIDC verification. |

Do not expose these values to client components or commit real values to Git.

## Project Configuration

Edit `rhapsody.config.ts` to point Rhapsody at your GitHub repository and Project board:

```ts
import type { RhapsodyProjectConfig } from "./lib/config";

export default {
  tracker: {
    kind: "github_project",
    owner: "your-org",
    repository: "your-repo",
    projectNumber: 1,
    statusField: "Status",
    activeStatuses: ["Todo", "In Progress"],
    terminalStatuses: ["Done", "Canceled", "Cancelled", "Duplicate"],
  },
  repository: {
    owner: "your-org",
    name: "your-repo",
    defaultBranch: "main",
    branchPrefix: "rhapsody/",
  },
  scheduler: {
    maxConcurrentRuns: 3,
    maxConcurrentRunsByStatus: {},
    maxRetryBackoffMs: 300000,
  },
  runner: {
    kind: "sandbox-codex",
    timeoutMs: 60 * 60 * 1000,
  },
} satisfies RhapsodyProjectConfig;
```

For the MVP, one Rhapsody deployment schedules one configured GitHub Project and repository.

## Repository Workflow Files

The target repository should contain `.rhapsody/INSTRUCTIONS.md`. This Markdown file is rendered into the Codex prompt for each claimed work item.

Example:

```md
# Rhapsody Instructions

You are working on this repository through Rhapsody.

Read the GitHub issue, inspect the existing code, make the smallest safe change, run relevant checks, and prepare a pull request.

Prefer clear commits, concise PR descriptions, and changes that a human maintainer can review quickly.
```

Optional repository policy can live in `.rhapsody/config.toml`, for example post-run review and auto-merge eligibility rules. See the repository's own `.rhapsody/config.toml` for a current example.

Codex runtime behavior should stay in Codex-native `.codex/` files. Rhapsody owns the sandbox, mediator, callback, and host-side safety constraints.

## Codex Credentials

Rhapsody is designed to run Codex inside Vercel Sandbox while keeping upstream ChatGPT-backed Codex credentials in trusted server-side storage.

For the MVP, operators seed the initial ChatGPT auth JSON from a local Codex login:

1. Log in to Codex locally.
2. Copy the contents of `~/.codex/auth.json`.
3. Set `INITIAL_CHATGPT_AUTH_JSON` in the trusted Rhapsody environment.
4. Call the admin seeding endpoint described in `.env.example`.
5. Remove the seed value after the current credentials have been stored and refresh health is confirmed.

This area is intentionally security-sensitive. Review [docs/SPEC.md](docs/SPEC.md) and the credential-related ADRs before exposing a deployment beyond trusted operators.

## Local Development

Install dependencies:

```bash
npm install
```

Run the development server:

```bash
npm run dev
```

Open [http://localhost:3000](http://localhost:3000).

Useful checks:

```bash
npm run lint
npm run typecheck
npm run test
npm run build
```

## Dashboard

The dashboard is protected by the configured root password. It is intended to help operators answer:

- Which work items are eligible?
- Which item is currently claimed?
- Which worker runs and attempts exist?
- What happened inside a run?
- Which branch or pull request was produced?
- Why did Rhapsody stop, retry, ask for human review, or release a claim?

The dashboard is an operational surface, not the primary workflow definition surface. The durable workflow policy should remain in Git and Markdown.

## Security Model

Rhapsody's MVP security model is intentionally narrow:

- Admin UI access is protected by `ROOT_PASSWORD` and signed session cookies.
- Cron and internal endpoints use shared secrets where applicable.
- GitHub writes are mediated by trusted server-side code.
- The upstream `GITHUB_TOKEN` stays in the Rhapsody deployment environment.
- Codex runs happen inside Vercel Sandbox.
- Secrets must not be logged or passed into arbitrary repository code.
- Generated code should be reviewed through normal pull request review.

This is not yet a hardened multi-tenant SaaS architecture. Treat a Rhapsody deployment as trusted team infrastructure.

     1	# Rhapsody
     2	
     3	Rhapsody is a serverless coding agent platform for teams that want GitHub Project issues to become observable Codex runs and pull requests.
     4	
     5	It started from excitement about [OpenAI Symphony](https://openai.com/index/open-source-codex-orchestration-symphony/) as a reference implementation for coding-agent orchestration. Rhapsody explores what that shape can look like when it is deployed like a Vercel application: durable workflows, isolated sandboxes, GitHub Projects as the work queue, and repository-owned Markdown as the workflow definition.
     6	
     7	The goal is to make team-facing coding-agent operations feel familiar:
     8	
     9	- GitHub Projects are the work queue.
    10	- `.rhapsody/INSTRUCTIONS.md` is the agent workflow prompt.
    11	- Codex-native `.codex/` files stay with the target repository.
    12	- Vercel Workflow coordinates scheduler and worker runs.
    13	- Vercel Sandbox isolates each Codex execution.
    14	- Rhapsody records claims, runs, attempts, events, decisions, branches, and pull requests.
    15	
    16	Rhapsody is a template and reference application, not a hosted SaaS. You deploy it into your own Vercel project and connect it to your own GitHub Project, repository, state store, and Codex credentials.
    17	
    18	## Why Rhapsody?
    19	
    20	Coding agents are most useful when they fit into the development workflow a team already trusts: issues, project boards, branches, pull requests, logs, and versioned instructions.
    21	
    22	Rhapsody is built around that idea. It does not try to move workflow policy into a separate product surface. The scheduler and runner live in a Vercel app, while the agent-facing instructions live in Git as Markdown.
    23	
    24	This is the shape Rhapsody is exploring:
    25	
    26	```text
    27	GitHub Project issue
    28	  -> scheduler workflow
    29	  -> durable claim
    30	  -> worker workflow
    31	  -> Vercel Sandbox
    32	  -> Codex
    33	  -> branch / pull request / dashboard events
    34	```
    35	
    36	Earlier Vercel and Next.js stacks made parts of this hard to model as a serverless application. With Workflow DevKit for durable orchestration and Vercel Sandbox for isolated execution, the pieces are much closer to the platform shape Rhapsody needs.
    37	
    38	## Current Status
    39	
    40	Rhapsody is an MVP. It is intended for people who are comfortable configuring GitHub Projects, Vercel projects, environment variables, and a durable database.
    41	
    42	The current implementation focuses on:
    43	
    44	- GitHub Projects v2 as the tracker.
    45	- A single configured GitHub repository and Project board.
    46	- Turso/libSQL as the state store.
    47	- Root-password admin access for the dashboard.
    48	- PAT-based GitHub access held by trusted Rhapsody server-side code.
    49	- Vercel Sandbox execution for Codex workers.
    50	- ChatGPT-backed Codex credential brokering for sandboxed Codex runs.
    51	- Dashboard and JSON API surfaces for operators.
    52	
    53	Known MVP boundaries:
    54	
    55	- Rhapsody is not multi-tenant.
    56	- GitHub App installation tokens are deferred; the MVP uses `GITHUB_TOKEN`.
    57	- GitHub Projects v2 is the only tracker.
    58	- Operators are expected to review generated pull requests.
    59	- Production-critical use requires careful review of the security model, credential handling, and sandbox policy.
    60	
    61	See [docs/SPEC.md](docs/SPEC.md) for the working product and engineering specification.
    62	
    63	## How It Works
    64	
    65	Rhapsody has three layers of configuration:
    66	
    67	1. Deployment configuration in environment variables.
    68	2. Scheduler configuration in `rhapsody.config.ts`.
    69	3. Repository workflow instructions in `.rhapsody/INSTRUCTIONS.md` and `.rhapsody/config.toml`.
    70	
    71	The scheduler polls or refreshes the configured GitHub Project, normalizes eligible ProjectV2 items into work items, and claims work in the durable state store. Worker workflows then create or restore a Vercel Sandbox, prepare the target repository, launch Codex with the rendered instructions, and record the resulting events and handoff artifacts.
    72	
    73	Rhapsody keeps sensitive credentials in trusted server-side code. Sandboxed runs use mediated access for GitHub writes and Codex credential access instead of receiving broad long-lived secrets directly.
    74	
    75	## Quick Start
    76	
    77	This is the expected happy path for a first run:
    78	
    79	1. Deploy Rhapsody to Vercel.
    80	2. Create a Turso/libSQL database for durable state.
    81	3. Create or choose a GitHub ProjectV2 board.
    82	4. Configure `rhapsody.config.ts` for your GitHub owner, repository, Project number, status field, and eligible statuses.
    83	5. Add the required environment variables in Vercel.
    84	6. Seed Codex ChatGPT credentials for sandboxed Codex runs.
    85	7. Add `.rhapsody/INSTRUCTIONS.md` to the target repository.
    86	8. Create one GitHub issue and place it in an eligible Project status.
    87	9. Open the Rhapsody dashboard.
    88	10. Trigger a manual scheduler refresh or wait for the configured cron.
    89	11. Watch Rhapsody create a run, execute Codex in Sandbox, and hand the result back as a branch and pull request.
    90	
    91	The setup experience is still being refined. Until the template flow and setup skill are complete, expect to read the configuration files and docs alongside this README.
    92	
    93	## Prerequisites
    94	
    95	You need:
    96	
    97	- A Vercel account with access to Workflow and Sandbox features.
    98	- A GitHub repository to run agents against.
    99	- A GitHub ProjectV2 board that contains the work items.
   100	- A GitHub token with repository access and ProjectV2 read/write access.
   101	- A Turso/libSQL database.
   102	- Codex credentials from a local ChatGPT login.
   103	- Node.js and npm for local development.
   104	
   105	## Environment Variables
   106	
   107	Copy `.env.example` and fill in the values locally, then add the same values to your Vercel project.
   108	
   109	Required for the MVP:
   110	
   111	| Name | Purpose |
   112	| --- | --- |
   113	| `ROOT_PASSWORD` | Password for the Rhapsody admin dashboard and bearer-protected admin endpoints. |
   114	| `AUTH_SECRET` | Secret used to sign admin session cookies. |
   115	| `TURSO_DATABASE_URL` | libSQL state store URL. |
   116	| `TURSO_AUTH_TOKEN` | libSQL auth token. |
   117	| `GITHUB_TOKEN` | Server-side GitHub credential for repository and ProjectV2 access. |
   118	| `MEDIATOR_SECRET` | Shared secret for sandbox-to-Rhapsody mediator calls. |
   119	| `VERCEL_TOKEN` | Vercel API token used for Sandbox operations. |
   120	| `VERCEL_TEAM_ID` | Vercel team scope for Sandbox operations. |
   121	| `VERCEL_PROJECT_ID` | Vercel project scope for Sandbox operations. |
   122	
   123	Common optional variables:
   124	
   125	| Name | Purpose |
   126	| --- | --- |
   127	| `CRON_SECRET` | Secret expected by cron/admin refresh endpoints. |
   128	| `INITIAL_CHATGPT_AUTH_JSON` | Initial Codex ChatGPT auth seed used by the credential seeding endpoint. |
   129	| `RHAPSODY_CODEX_BASE_SNAPSHOT_ID` | Optional Sandbox snapshot ID used as a Codex-ready base image. |
   130	| `VERCEL_PROTECTION_BYPASS_SECRET` | Optional Vercel Deployment Protection bypass for callback brokering. |
   131	| `VERCEL_OIDC_ISSUER` | Optional issuer for Vercel OIDC verification. |
   132	| `VERCEL_OIDC_AUDIENCE` | Optional audience for Vercel OIDC verification. |
   133	| `VERCEL_TEAM_SLUG` | Optional team slug for Vercel OIDC verification. |
   134	
   135	Do not expose these values to client components or commit real values to Git.
   136	
   137	## Project Configuration
   138	
   139	Edit `rhapsody.config.ts` to point Rhapsody at your GitHub repository and Project board:
   140	
   141	```ts
   142	import type { RhapsodyProjectConfig } from "./lib/config";
   143	
   144	export default {
   145	  tracker: {
   146	    kind: "github_project",
   147	    owner: "your-org",
   148	    repository: "your-repo",
   149	    projectNumber: 1,
   150	    statusField: "Status",
   151	    activeStatuses: ["Todo", "In Progress"],
   152	    terminalStatuses: ["Done", "Canceled", "Cancelled", "Duplicate"],
   153	  },
   154	  repository: {
   155	    owner: "your-org",
   156	    name: "your-repo",
   157	    defaultBranch: "main",
   158	    branchPrefix: "rhapsody/",
   159	  },
   160	  scheduler: {
   161	    maxConcurrentRuns: 3,
   162	    maxConcurrentRunsByStatus: {},
   163	    maxRetryBackoffMs: 300000,
   164	  },
   165	  runner: {
   166	    kind: "sandbox-codex",
   167	    timeoutMs: 60 * 60 * 1000,
   168	  },
   169	} satisfies RhapsodyProjectConfig;
   170	```
   171	
   172	For the MVP, one Rhapsody deployment schedules one configured GitHub Project and repository.
   173	
   174	## Repository Workflow Files
   175	
   176	The target repository should contain `.rhapsody/INSTRUCTIONS.md`. This Markdown file is rendered into the Codex prompt for each claimed work item.
   177	
   178	Example:
   179	
   180	```md
   181	# Rhapsody Instructions
   182	
   183	You are working on this repository through Rhapsody.
   184	
   185	Read the GitHub issue, inspect the existing code, make the smallest safe change, run relevant checks, and prepare a pull request.
   186	
   187	Prefer clear commits, concise PR descriptions, and changes that a human maintainer can review quickly.
   188	```
   189	
   190	Optional repository policy can live in `.rhapsody/config.toml`, for example post-run review and auto-merge eligibility rules. See the repository's own `.rhapsody/config.toml` for a current example.
   191	
   192	Codex runtime behavior should stay in Codex-native `.codex/` files. Rhapsody owns the sandbox, mediator, callback, and host-side safety constraints.
   193	
   194	## Codex Credentials
   195	
   196	Rhapsody is designed to run Codex inside Vercel Sandbox while keeping upstream ChatGPT-backed Codex credentials in trusted server-side storage.
   197	
   198	For the MVP, operators seed the initial ChatGPT auth JSON from a local Codex login:
   199	
   200	1. Log in to Codex locally.
   201	2. Copy the contents of `~/.codex/auth.json`.
   202	3. Set `INITIAL_CHATGPT_AUTH_JSON` in the trusted Rhapsody environment.
   203	4. Call the admin seeding endpoint described in `.env.example`.
   204	5. Remove the seed value after the current credentials have been stored and refresh health is confirmed.
   205	
   206	This area is intentionally security-sensitive. Review [docs/SPEC.md](docs/SPEC.md) and the credential-related ADRs before exposing a deployment beyond trusted operators.
   207	
   208	## Local Development
   209	
   210	Install dependencies:
   211	
   212	```bash
   213	npm install
   214	```
   215	
   216	Run the development server:
   217	
   218	```bash
   219	npm run dev
   220	```
   221	
   222	Open [http://localhost:3000](http://localhost:3000).
   223	
   224	Useful checks:
   225	
   226	```bash
   227	npm run lint
   228	npm run typecheck
   229	npm run test
   230	npm run build
   231	```
   232	
   233	## Dashboard
   234	
   235	The dashboard is protected by the configured root password. It is intended to help operators answer:
   236	
   237	- Which work items are eligible?
   238	- Which item is currently claimed?
   239	- Which worker runs and attempts exist?
   240	- What happened inside a run?
   241	- Which branch or pull request was produced?
   242	- Why did Rhapsody stop, retry, ask for human review, or release a claim?
   243	
   244	The dashboard is an operational surface, not the primary workflow definition surface. The durable workflow policy should remain in Git and Markdown.
   245	
   246	## Security Model
   247	
   248	Rhapsody's MVP security model is intentionally narrow:
   249	
   250	- Admin UI access is protected by `ROOT_PASSWORD` and signed session cookies.
   251	- Cron and internal endpoints use shared secrets where applicable.
   252	- GitHub writes are mediated by trusted server-side code.
   253	- The upstream `GITHUB_TOKEN` stays in the Rhapsody deployment environment.
   254	- Codex runs happen inside Vercel Sandbox.
   255	- Secrets must not be logged or passed into arbitrary repository code.
   256	- Generated code should be reviewed through normal pull request review.
   257	
   258	This is not yet a hardened multi-tenant SaaS architecture. Treat a Rhapsody deployment as trusted team infrastructure.
   259	
   260	## Documentation
   261	
   262	- [docs/SPEC.md](docs/SPEC.md): working product and engineering specification.
   263	- [docs/CONCEPTS.md](docs/CONCEPTS.md): core domain concepts.
   264	- [docs/ORIGINAL_SPEC.md](docs/ORIGINAL_SPEC.md): unmodified Symphony reference spec.
   265	- [docs/adr](docs/adr): architecture decisions.
   266	
   267	## Roadmap
   268	
   269	Near-term work:
   270	
   271	- Publish a Vercel Template flow.
   272	- Add a setup skill for target repositories.
   273	- Improve the first-run setup guide.
   274	- Add clearer database initialization docs.
   275	- Add a setup/doctor command for environment and Project validation.
   276	- Add screenshots and a short demo walkthrough.
   277	- Continue hardening credential refresh, mediator authorization, and sandbox policy.
   278	
   279	Later work:
   280	
   281	- GitHub App support.
   282	- Multiple projects or repositories per deployment.
   283	- More tracker adapters.
   284	- Richer repair and review workers.
   285	- Better hosted-template onboarding.
   286	
   287	## License
   288	
   289	License information is not finalized yet.

docs/CONCEPTS.md:3:Rhapsody is a Vercel-native agent runner that turns GitHub Project items into sandboxed Codex runs.
docs/CONCEPTS.md:23:inside the sandbox while Rhapsody still owns sandbox, network, credential, and verification
docs/CONCEPTS.md:34:An Attempt is one execution of a Run inside one Vercel Sandbox.
docs/CONCEPTS.md:41:A Runner Workflow is a durable workflow that prepares one Attempt, starts sandboxed execution, waits
docs/CONCEPTS.md:44:A Vercel Sandbox is the isolation boundary where Codex may edit files and run commands.
docs/CONCEPTS.md:46:Source Initialization is the process that prepares the target repository inside the Vercel Sandbox
docs/CONCEPTS.md:49:A Sandbox Wrapper is the Rhapsody-owned command that runs inside the Vercel Sandbox, launches Codex,
docs/CONCEPTS.md:55:A Terminal Callback is the message from the Sandbox Wrapper that tells the Runner Workflow that
docs/CONCEPTS.md:56:sandboxed execution has completed, failed, timed out, or stopped.
docs/CONCEPTS.md:58:Execution Status is the observed result of sandboxed command execution.
docs/CONCEPTS.md:64:A Mediator is a trusted Rhapsody endpoint that lets sandboxed Codex perform approved external side
docs/CONCEPTS.md:77:are not written into the sandbox filesystem, command arguments, logs, callback payloads, exports, or
docs/CONCEPTS.md:99:An Event is an append-only record of scheduler, runner, sandbox, mediator, verification, or
docs/CONCEPTS.md:102:Dashboard State is the queryable projection of Claims, Runs, Attempts, Events, sandbox references,
docs/CONCEPTS.md:109:expired Attempts, stopped sandboxes, and Work Items that moved outside the active Project Boundary.

{"status":{"019e6f31-792e-7792-921e-14f485c1aca1":{"completed":"読みました。ファイル編集はしていません。\n\n**Overall Take**\n\nREADME is already compelling as a strategic introduction. It clearly positions Rhapsody in the right emotional neighborhood: Symphony-inspired, Vercel-native, GitHub Projects-driven, Markdown-owned, sandboxed Codex execution.\n\nThe main issue is that it currently reads more like a strong product/design brief than a launch-ready “I clicked from X, now what?” README. That is acceptable for now, but if this is the first public touchpoint, it needs sharper separation between:\n\n- what works today\n- what is intended soon\n- what the reader can try in 15-30 minutes\n- what remains architectural aspiration\n\n**What Is Compelling**\n\nThe opening sentence is strong:\n\n> Rhapsody is a serverless coding agent platform for teams that want GitHub Project issues to become observable Codex runs and pull requests.\n\nThis is concrete and target-reader friendly. “Observable Codex runs and pull requests” is much better than generic “AI agent automation.”\n\nThe Symphony paragraph works well. It respectfully frames Rhapsody as inspired by Symphony without making it sound like a replacement or critique.\n\nThe bullets under “team-facing coding-agent operations feel familiar” are good. They quickly communicate the product taste: GitHub Projects, Markdown, Codex files, Workflow, Sandbox, durable records.\n\nThe “not a hosted SaaS” sentence is important and should stay near the top.\n\n**What Is Confusing**\n\nThe biggest confusion: “worker workflow” vs “runner workflow” vs “worker runs” vs “Codex workers” are used somewhat interchangeably. A new reader may wonder whether these are distinct concepts.\n\nRecommendation: choose one product term, probably `runner`, and use it consistently.\n\nCurrent:\n\n```text\n-> worker workflow\n```\n\nMaybe:\n\n```text\n-> runner workflow\n```\n\nThe README says `.rhapsody/INSTRUCTIONS.md` is “the agent workflow prompt,” but also says “Codex-native `.codex/` files stay with the target repository.” This is conceptually right, but a target reader may ask: what goes in `.rhapsody/` vs `.codex/`?\n\nAdd a small table:\n\n| File | Owned by | Purpose |\n| --- | --- | --- |\n| `.rhapsody/INSTRUCTIONS.md` | Rhapsody | Per-run workflow instructions rendered with issue context |\n| `.rhapsody/config.toml` | Rhapsody | Repository policy for review/merge/handoff |\n| `.codex/*` | Codex | Codex runtime behavior and local agent preferences |\n\nThe “Codex credentials from a local ChatGPT login” prerequisite is interesting but also alarming. It needs one extra plain-English sentence before the detailed section:\n\n> This is currently an operator-managed MVP flow, not a polished OAuth installation flow.\n\nThat would reduce surprise.\n\n**Too Aspirational Or Vague**\n\n“ChatGPT-backed Codex credential brokering for sandboxed Codex runs” sounds advanced, but it is not clear whether it is already implemented, partially implemented, or planned.\n\nIf implemented, say:\n\n> Implemented for trusted single-tenant deployments.\n\nIf partial, say:\n\n> Partially implemented; the setup flow is still manual.\n\nSimilarly:\n\n> Rhapsody records claims, runs, attempts, events, decisions, branches, and pull requests.\n\nThis sounds complete. If any of these are still incomplete, add qualifiers. Public readers forgive MVPs; they dislike discovering that polished words meant “planned.”\n\nThe roadmap contains “Publish a Vercel Template flow,” while the intro says “Rhapsody is a template and reference application.” That creates tension. Is it already a template, or becoming one?\n\nSuggested wording:\n\n> Rhapsody is intended to be distributed as a Vercel Template and currently works as a reference application you can deploy yourself.\n\n**Missing Before Someone Tries It**\n\nThe README needs a very explicit “Can I try this today?” box near the top.\n\nSuggested section after Current Status:\n\n```md\n## Can I Try It Today?\n\nYes, if you are comfortable wiring together Vercel, GitHub Projects v2, Turso/libSQL, and Codex credentials manually.\n\nThe fully guided Vercel Template and setup skill are still in progress. For now, expect to configure environment variables, edit `rhapsody.config.ts`, initialize the database, and seed Codex credentials by hand.\n```\n\nIt also needs a “What you will get after first run” section:\n\n```md\nAfter a successful first run, you should see:\n\n- A claimed GitHub Project item in the Rhapsody dashboard.\n- A recorded runner attempt with event logs.\n- A branch created in the target repository.\n- A pull request or handoff artifact linked from the run detail page.\n```\n\nThe Quick Start says “Deploy Rhapsody to Vercel” and “Create a Turso/libSQL database,” but does not link to exact docs or commands. For launch, at least these need concrete links or placeholders:\n\n- how to initialize schema\n- what admin seeding endpoint path is\n- what URL path the dashboard lives at\n- what URL path triggers manual refresh\n- whether Vercel Workflow/Sandbox are public/beta/paid/limited\n- whether `VERCEL_PROJECT_ID` is the Rhapsody app project or target repo project\n\n**Structure Improvements**\n\nI would move “Why Rhapsody?” before the implementation bullet list, but keep the first short definition at the top.\n\nProposed top structure:\n\n1. `# Rhapsody`\n2. One-sentence definition\n3. Short origin story: Symphony excitement, serverless/Vercel experiment, Git+Markdown workflow\n4. “What it does” bullets\n5. “Can I try it today?”\n6. “How it works”\n7. “Quick start”\n\nRight now the opening is good, but it quickly becomes architectural. A launch reader needs emotional context first, then architecture.\n\nI would also split “Current Status” into two sections:\n\n- `What Works Today`\n- `MVP Boundaries`\n\nThat makes the trust boundary cleaner.\n\n**Concrete Wording Suggestions**\n\nOpening paragraph could be warmer and more launch-oriented:\n\n```md\nRhapsody started from excitement about OpenAI Symphony: a compelling reference implementation for coding-agent orchestration.\n\nThe question behind Rhapsody is: what would that shape look like if it were serverless, Vercel-native, and easy to deploy as a template?\n\nRhapsody uses GitHub Projects as the work queue, Markdown as the workflow definition, Vercel Workflow DevKit for durable orchestration, Vercel Sandbox for isolated execution, and Codex as the coding agent.\n```\n\nCurrent:\n\n> Earlier Vercel and Next.js stacks made parts of this hard to model as a serverless application.\n\nThis is good but slightly vague. Sharpen it:\n\n```md\nLong-running schedulers and isolated agent execution are awkward fits for a traditional request/response Next.js app. Workflow DevKit and Vercel Sandbox make those pieces easier to model as durable, serverless infrastructure.\n```\n\nCurrent:\n\n> The setup experience is still being refined.\n\nGood honesty, but too soft. Make it practical:\n\n```md\nThe guided setup flow is not finished yet. Until the Vercel Template and setup skill are complete, this README is best treated as an operator setup guide for early adopters.\n```\n\n**Launch Communications Angle**\n\nFor SNS/community readers, README should surface the “why this exists” slightly more:\n\n- Symphony showed the orchestration shape.\n- Vercel Workflow/Sandbox make a serverless version plausible.\n- Notion Dev-like workflows are interesting, but Rhapsody keeps workflow definitions in Git and Markdown.\n- The output is not chat; it is branches, PRs, logs, and reviewable work.\n\nNotion Dev is not mentioned in the README. I think it should be mentioned carefully, maybe once, without comparison drama:\n\n```md\nRhapsody is also interested in the same broad future as tools like Notion Dev: coding agents that participate in real team workflows. Its bet is that developer-facing workflow policy should stay close to the repository, in Git and Markdown.\n```\n\nThat sentence would help the intended audience recognize themselves.\n\n**Highest-Impact Fixes Before Launch**\n\n1. Add “Can I try it today?” with honest readiness level.\n2. Clarify Vercel Template status: available now vs planned.\n3. Add exact first-run success criteria.\n4. Add `.rhapsody/` vs `.codex/` distinction table.\n5. Make Codex credential setup sound intentionally early/manual, not mysteriously magical.\n6. Replace worker/runner inconsistency with one term.\n7. Mention Notion Dev-like motivation once, framed positively.\n8. Add concrete dashboard/manual refresh/seeding endpoint paths once they are known.\n\nThe README has a good soul already. It just needs a little less mist around the sharp edges, because the target audience is exactly the kind of person who will get excited and then immediately ask, “Okay, can I actually run this tonight?”"}},"timed_out":false}

For launch, at least these need concrete links or placeholders:\n\n- how to initialize schema\n- what admin seeding endpoint path is\n- what URL path the dashboard lives at\n- what URL path triggers manual refresh\n- whether Vercel Workflow/Sandbox are public/beta/paid/limited\n- whether `VERCEL_PROJECT_ID` is the Rhapsody app project or target repo project\n\n**Structure Improvements**\n\nI would move “Why Rhapsody?” before the implementation bullet list, but keep the first short definition at the top.\n\nProposed top structure:\n\n1. `# Rhapsody`\n2. One-sentence definition\n3. Short origin story: Symphony excitement, serverless/Vercel experiment, Git+Markdown workflow\n4. “What it does” bullets\n5. “Can I try it today?”\n6. “How it works”\n7. “Quick start”\n\nRight now the opening is good, but it quickly becomes architectural. A launch reader needs emotional context first, then architecture.\n\nI would also split “Current Status” into two sections:\n\n- `What Works Today`\n- `MVP Boundaries`\n\nThat makes the trust boundary cleaner.\n\n**Concrete Wording Suggestions**\n\nOpening paragraph could be warmer and more launch-oriented:\n\n```md\nRhapsody started from excitement about OpenAI Symphony: a compelling reference implementation for coding-agent orchestration.\n\nThe question behind Rhapsody is: what would that shape look like if it were serverless, Vercel-native, and easy to deploy as a template?\n\nRhapsody uses GitHub Projects as the work queue, Markdown as the workflow definition, Vercel Workflow DevKit for durable orchestration, Vercel Sandbox for isolated execution, and Codex as the coding agent.\n```\n\nCurrent:\n\n> Earlier Vercel and Next.js stacks made parts of this hard to model as a serverless application.\n\nThis is good but slightly vague. Sharpen it:\n\n```md\nLong-running schedulers and isolated agent execution are awkward fits for a traditional request/response Next.js app. Workflow DevKit and Vercel Sandbox make those pieces easier to model as durable, serverless infrastructure.\n```\n\nCurrent:\n\n> The setup experience is still being refined.\n\nGood honesty, but too soft. Make it practical:\n\n```md\nThe guided setup flow is not finished yet. Until the Vercel Template and setup skill are complete, this README is best treated as an operator setup guide for early adopters.\n```\n\n**Launch Communications Angle**\n\nFor SNS/community readers, README should surface the “why this exists” slightly more:\n\n- Symphony showed the orchestration shape.\n- Vercel Workflow/Sandbox make a serverless version plausible.\n- Notion Dev-like workflows are interesting, but Rhapsody keeps workflow definitions in Git and Markdown.\n- The output is not chat; it is branches, PRs, logs, and reviewable work.\n\nNotion Dev is not mentioned in the README. I think it should be mentioned carefully, maybe once, without comparison drama:\n\n```md\nRhapsody is also interested in the same broad future as tools like Notion Dev: coding agents that participate in real team workflows. Its bet is that developer-facing workflow policy should stay close to the repository, in Git and Markdown.\n```\n\nThat sentence would help the intended audience recognize themselves.\n\n**Highest-Impact Fixes Before Launch**\n\n1. Add “Can I try it today?” with honest readiness level.\n2. Clarify Vercel Template status: available now vs planned.\n3. Add exact first-run success criteria.\n4. Add `.rhapsody/` vs `.codex/` distinction table.\n5. Make Codex credential setup sound intentionally early/manual, not mysteriously magical.\n6. Replace worker/runner inconsistency with one term.\n7. Mention Notion Dev-like motivation once, framed positively.\n8. Add concrete dashboard/manual refresh/seeding endpoint paths once they are known.\n\nThe README has a good soul already. It just needs a little less mist around the sharp edges, because the target audience is exactly the kind of person who will get excited and then immediately ask, “Okay, can I actually run this tonight?”"}}

> AGENT

はい、かなり良いcriticになりました。まとめると、今のREADMEは「思想・公開メッセージ」としては良いけど、「初回実行ガイド」としてはまだ信頼を落としうる、という評価です。

**一番大きい指摘**

最大の問題は、`.rhapsody/INSTRUCTIONS.md` の場所です。

READMEでは「target repositoryに置く」と書いていますが、実装上は現時点で `process.cwd()`、つまりRhapsodyアプリのcheckout側から読んでいるようです。これが本当なら、ユーザーが対象repoに `.rhapsody/INSTRUCTIONS.md` を置いても反映されない可能性があります。

これは公開前にどちらかへ揃えるべきです。

- 実装をSPEC通り「target repoから読む」に直す
- いったんREADMEを「current MVP reads from the Rhapsody deployment checkout」と正直にする

プロダクト方向としては前者がよさそうです。

**READMEとして直したいこと**

1. **“Can I try it today?” を追加する**
   今すぐ試せるが、手動設定が必要、という温度感を明示する。

2. **Template statusを曖昧にしない**
   今は「template and reference application」と「Vercel Template flowはRoadmap」が衝突しています。
   “intended to be distributed as a Vercel Template; currently a reference app” が安全です。

3. **実行に必要な具体endpointを書く**
   実装には以下があるようです。
   - `POST /api/v1/admin/db/migrate`
   - `POST /api/v1/admin/codex-chatgpt-credentials/seed-from-env`
   - `POST/GET /api/v1/admin/scheduler/tick`

   READMEのQuick Startにこれがないので、初回runが guesswork になります。

4. **dashboardでscheduler refreshできるように読める表現を直す**
   現状dashboardには「view refresh」はあるが、scheduler tickボタンはなさそうです。
   READMEは「admin scheduler endpointを呼ぶ」と書くほうが正確。

5. **`INITIAL_CHATGPT_AUTH_JSON` をoptional扱いしない**
   `sandbox-codex` がデフォルトなら、happy pathでは実質必須です。
   “Required for `sandbox-codex`” に分けるべき。

6. **GitHub mediator/securityの表現を少し弱める**
   READMEは「broad secretsをsandboxに渡さない」と言い切っていますが、実装にはまだexploratoryなGitHub token transformがあるようです。
   ここは「intended model」「current MVP still has transitional credential paths」みたいにしたほうが信頼できます。

7. **CodexのPR handoff contractを書く**
   wrapperは、Codexがcommit/pushして `/vercel/sandbox/rhapsody-output/pr.json` を書くことを期待しているようです。
   これは `.rhapsody/INSTRUCTIONS.md` の例に含めるべきです。

8. **`.rhapsody/` と `.codex/` の役割分担表を追加する**
   初見だとここは混乱しやすいです。

9. **Notion Devの動機を1回だけ入れる**
   攻撃ではなく、
   “Rhapsody is interested in the same broad future as tools like Notion Dev, but keeps workflow policy in Git and Markdown.”
   くらいが良さそうです。

10. **品質チェックに `npm run format:check` を足す**
   小さいけど公開repoとして整います。

**僕の判断**

READMEはこのままでも「思想」は伝わります。ただ、公開して興味を持った人が本当に動かそうとしたとき、次の3つで詰まりそうです。

- `.rhapsody/INSTRUCTIONS.md` をどこに置くのか
- DB migration / credential seed / scheduler tick をどう呼ぶのか
- “PRができる” ためにCodexが満たすべき出力契約は何か

なので次の一手は、READMEをさらに磨くより先に、`.rhapsody/INSTRUCTIONS.md` の読み込み元を実装としてどうしたいか決めるのがよさそうです。方向が決まったら、READMEを「early adopter向けに正確なQuick Start」へ寄せて更新できます。
